A new understanding of grazing limit
Tong Yang, Yu-long Zhou

Abstract
. The grazing limit of the Boltzmann equation to Landau equation is well-known and has been justified by using cutoff near the grazing angle with some suitable scaling. In this paper, we will provide a new understanding by simply applying a natural scaling on the Boltzmann operator without angular cutoff. The proof is based on a new well-posedness theory on the Boltzmann equation without angular cutoff in the regime with optimal ranges of parameters so that the grazing limit can be justified directly for any γ > − 5 \gamma>-5 that includes the Coulomb potential corresponding to γ = − 3 \gamma=-3 . With this new understanding, the scaled Boltzmann operator in fact can be decomposed into two components. The first one converges to the Landau operator when the singular parameter s s of interaction angle tends to 1 − 1^{-} and the second one vanishes in this limit.



AMS Subject Classification (2010):
35Q20, 35R11, 75P05.

1.
Introduction

The Boltzmann and Landau equations are the two most classical kinetic equations. Regarding to
the Cauchy problem,
there has been extensive work in different frameworks, e.g.
[
29
,
30
,
5
,
6
,
10
,
25
,
31
,
32
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
9
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
15
,
2
,
19
,
22
,
13
]
. In fact, the Landau equation was derived by Landau in 1936
from the Boltzmann equation with cutoff Rutherford
cross section. Mathematical justification of the grazing collision limit has proved to be successful
since 1990s by adding a cutoff angle with suitable scaling parameter to the Boltzmann
cross-section,
cf.
[
3
,
4
,
7
,
8
,
14
,
20
,
21
,
23
,
12
]
.

This paper aims to provide a new approach to justisfy this limit so that the relation between the
Boltzmann equation and Landau equation can be understood from a different angle. Precisely, we study the grazing limit directly starting from the Boltzmann equation with angular non-cutoff kernels
B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\sim\theta^{-2-2s}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}

originating from the
inverse power law potentials. Roughly speaking,
with a proper scaling to the Boltzmann cross-section, when parameter of
the angular singularity
s\to 1^{-}
,
we naturally justify the limit to the Landau equation for any
\gamma>-5
. We point out
that this new approach is related to but very different from the existing arguments for grazing limit.

1.1.
A natural scaling

Consider the Cauchy problem of the Landau equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=Q_{L}^{\gamma}(F,F),~~t>0,x\in\mathbb{T}^{3},v\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3},\\
&F|_{t=0}=F_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

Here the Landau operator
Q_{L}^{\gamma}(g,h)
is defined by

\displaystyle Q_{L}^{\gamma}(g,h)(v):=\nabla_{v}\cdot\bigg\{\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}a^{\gamma}(v-v_{*})[g(v_{*})\nabla_{v}h(v)-\nabla_{v_{*}}g(v_{*})h(v)]\mathrm{d}v_{*}\bigg\}.

(1.2)

where the symmetric matrix
a^{\gamma}
is given by

\displaystyle a^{\gamma}(z)=\Lambda|z|^{\gamma+2}\Pi(z),\quad\Pi(z):=(I_{3}-\frac{z\otimes z}{|z|^{2}}).

(1.3)

Here,
I_{3}
is the
3\times 3
identity matrix and
\Lambda
is a constant.

We will show that the solution
F_{L}^{\gamma}
of (
1.1
) can be derived from that of the Boltzmann equation with a natural scaling.
Let
F^{s,\gamma}_{B}
be the solution to
the Boltzmann equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}(F,F),\quad F|_{t=0}=(1-s)F_{0},

(1.4)

where
Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}
is the Boltzmann operator defined by

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}(g,h)(v):=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h\right)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(1.5)

Here,
B^{s,\gamma}
is the angular non-cutoff kernel derived from inverse power law potentials, given by

\displaystyle B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)=C_{s,\gamma}(\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{-2-2s}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}.

(1.6)

The main result in this paper is to rigorously prove for
\gamma>-5
,

\displaystyle F^{s,\gamma}_{B}/(1-s)\to F_{L}^{\gamma},\quad s\to 1^{-}.

(1.7)

This can be stated mathematically as that the Boltzmann equation with a proper scaling tends to the Landau equation as
s\to 1^{-}
.

Recall that (
1.6
) is derived from the inverse power law potential
U(r)=r^{-p}
. For
p\geq 1
, one has
s=1/p,\gamma=1-4s
. The Coulomb potential
corresponds to
p=s=1,\gamma=-3
. In order to treat the Coulomb potential physically, we can take
\gamma=1-4s
and study the limit
s\to 1^{-}
.
Our result directly yield limit to the Landau equation with
\gamma=-3
in (
1.7
).
To be more general in mathematics, in the following discussion, we will regard
\gamma
as a fixed constant in the range
\gamma>-5
for Landau and
\gamma>-3-2s
for Boltzmann.

In the existing literatures on the grazing limit, for instance in
[
8
]
,
by introducing a
cutoff
\theta\gtrsim\epsilon

and by suitably adding the scaling factor
\epsilon^{2s-2}
, Desvillettes
considered the scaled Boltzmann kernel

B^{\epsilon,s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)=\epsilon^{2s-2}(\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{-2-2s}\mathrm{1}_{0\leq\theta\leq\epsilon}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}
and studied the limit
\epsilon\to 0
. See also
[
20
]
and
[
12
]
for further development
in this direction.
However, this kind of revised artificial Boltzmann kernels do not correspond to any physical potential as shown in Figure
1
.

We now explain Figure
1
. Let
\gamma>-5
be fixed.
In the middle column, the Landau equation
with initial datum
F_{0}
yields a solution
F^{\gamma}_{L}
.
Our new approach corresponds to the right column in Figure
1
.
That is, the Boltzmann equation with the scaled initial datum
(1-s)F_{0}
directly
gives a solution
F^{s,\gamma}_{B}
. Then the scaling
F^{s,\gamma}_{B}/(1-s)
with limit
s\to 1^{-}
gives the solution
F^{\gamma}_{L}
of the Landau equation.
Note that the Boltzmann equation is considered with the physical cross-section (
1.6
) with parameter
s
. We also remark that in the previous
argument for grazing limit, the parameter
s
is fixed while the artificial cutoff parameter
\epsilon
plays a role in the limit. However, in our approach, the limit of
s
to
1
yields the
limit of the Boltzmann equation to Landau equation.

Figure 1.
Grazing limit VS our method

In the following, we will explain in more details about the new appraoch.
Let
\tilde{F}^{s,\gamma}_{B}=F^{s,\gamma}_{B}/(1-s)
. Then
\tilde{F}^{s,\gamma}_{B}
is the solution to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=\tilde{Q}^{s,\gamma}_{B}(F,F),\quad F|_{t=0}=F_{0}.

(1.8)

Here
\tilde{Q}^{s,\gamma}_{B}
is the Boltzmann operator defined with the kernel

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)=(1-s)B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma).

(1.9)

In order to prove the limit (
1.7
), it is equivalent to
prove that

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{s,\gamma}_{B}\to F_{L}^{\gamma},\quad s\to 1^{-}.

(1.10)

The factor
(1-s)
that naturally appears
in (
1.9
) corresponds to the grazing limit. This is because only small deviation collisions contribute in the limit

s\to 1^{-}
.

In this paper, we will investigate
the above limit in the near-equilibrium framework where the
unique global classical solution can be constructed. We first recall some relevant results on the
well-posedness theories in this framework.
For the non-cutoff kernel (
1.6
),
Gressman-Strain in
[
15
]
established global well-posedness of the Boltzmann equation
in the following parameter range

\displaystyle\gamma>-3,\quad 0<s<1.

(1.11)

Independently, Alexandre-Morimoto-Ukai-Xu-Yang
[
2
]
proved the same result in the range (
1.11
) with a constraint
\gamma+2s>-\frac{3}{2}
to obtain a better estimate on the nonlinear operators. In order to consider the grazing limit for Coulomb potential
\gamma=-3
, we need to obtain some uniform estimates for
\gamma<-3
.
There are some discussions about this
in the previous works. For instance, the weak solutions were constructed for

\gamma\geq-3
for the two types of equation in the classical work
[
32
]

by Villani in which a remark on page 284 says that ’one could take
\gamma>-4
’.
As for Landau equation, Guo
[
16
]
firstly proved global well-posedness of
for
\gamma\geq-3
and he pointed out on page 394 in
[
16
]
that
’Although our theorem is still valid for certain

\gamma

even below
-3
’.

Motivated by the above works, we will consider the range

\gamma\leq-3
in this paper.
The new contribution of this paper to the Boltzmann equation
is to contain the parameters range for
\gamma
and
s
in the triangle
0<s<1,-3-2s<\gamma\leq-3
, see the region formed by the three red dash lines in Figure
2
.
On one hand, we justisfy the well-posedness of the Boltzmann equation in a region below

\gamma=-3
.
On the other hand, more importantly, the uniform estimates are obtained to the left of the vertical line
s=1
so that the limit
s\to 1^{-}
can be considered for any
\gamma>-5
. This then
obvious includes the Coulomb potential
\gamma=-3
and the cases mentioned in
the previous literatures. Hence, as a byproduct, our new contribution for the Landau equation
is the well-posedness for
-5<\gamma<-3
.

Figure 2.
The parameters domain

Now let us explain why
\gamma=-2s-3
is a valid threshold in three dimensional space. It is now well known that
the Boltzmann operator
Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}(g,\cdot)
in (
1.5
) behaves like
a fractional Laplace operator
-C_{g}(-\Delta)^{s}
. Recall that
-(-\Delta)^{s}
in three dimensions can be defined by a
singular integral

\displaystyle-(-\Delta)^{s}f(v):=C_{s}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B(v,r)}\frac{f(v)-f(v_{*})}{|v-v_{*}|^{3+2s}}\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(1.12)

This implies that
\gamma>-2s-3
for suitably general function spaces. Moreover,
there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}\frac{C_{s}}{1-s}=c.

This implies the scaling factor
1-s
naturally appears in (
1.7
). Then as
s\to 1^{-}
,

-(-\Delta)^{s}\to\Delta
which is the main part of
the Landau operator (
1.2
).

1.2.
Main results

We will state the main results in this subsection. Consider
the following Cauchy problem of the Boltzmann equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}(F,F),~~t>0,x\in\mathbb{T}^{3},v\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3},\\
&F|_{t=0}=F_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.13)

Here
F(t,x,v)\geq 0
is the density
function of particles with velocity

v\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}
at time
t\geq 0
, position
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}:=[-\pi,\pi]^{3}
. The Boltzmann operator is defined as

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}(g,h)(v):=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\left(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h\right)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(1.14)

Here,
h=h(v)
,
g_{*}=g(v_{*})
,

h^{\prime}=h(v^{\prime})
,
g^{\prime}_{*}=g(v^{\prime}_{*})
where
v^{\prime}
,
v_{*}^{\prime}
are given by

\displaystyle v^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\quad v^{\prime}_{*}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}-\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\quad\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{2}.

(1.15)

Recalling (
1.6
) and (
1.9
), from now on
we take

\displaystyle B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)=(1-s)(\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{-2-2s}\mathrm{1}_{0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma},

(1.16)

where
\cos\theta=\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\cdot\sigma
. That is,
the angular function is

\displaystyle b^{s}(\theta)=(1-s)(\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{-2-2s}\mathrm{1}_{0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2}.

Note that the angle variable is restricted to

0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2
by symmetry as other papers.
Thanks to the factor
1-s
, the mean moment transfer is finite
by computing

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b^{s}(\theta)\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma=4\pi\times 2^{s-1}.

(1.17)

In accordance with (
1.17
), we take
\Lambda=\pi
in (
1.3
).
In fact, we will show that

Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}=2^{s-1}Q^{\gamma}_{L}+O(1-s),

and so

Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}\to Q^{\gamma}_{L}
as
s\to 1^{-}
. See (
7.7
) for details.

To construct the global-in-time classical solution in the spatially inhomogeneous case, one usually consider the near equilibrium framework.
Recall that the solution of (
1.1
)
and (
1.13
) conserves mass, momentum and energy. We assume
F_{0}
is
a small perturbation of the equilibrium
\mu
where
\mu(v):=(2\pi)^{-3/2}e^{-|v|^{2}/2}
.
Let us recall the linearized versions of (
1.13
) and (
1.1
).
Set
F=\mu+\mu^{1/2}f
, then (
1.13
) is reduced to

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{B}f=\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{B}(f,f),~~t>0,x\in\mathbb{T}^{3},v\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3},\\
&f|_{t=0}=f_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.18)

Here the linearized Boltzmann operator
\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{B}
and the nonlinear term
\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{B}
are defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{B}(g,h):=\mu^{-1/2}Q^{s,\gamma}_{B}(\mu^{1/2}g,\mu^{1/2}h),\quad\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{B}g:=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{B}(\mu^{1/2},g)-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{B}(g,\mu^{1/2}).

(1.19)

With the same decomposition, set
F=\mu+\mu^{1/2}f
. Then (
1.1
) becomes

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\mathcal{L}_{L}^{\gamma}f=\Gamma_{L}^{\gamma}(f,f),t>0,x\in\mathbb{T}^{3},v\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3},\\
&f|_{t=0}=f_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.20)

The linearized Landau operator
\mathcal{L}_{L}^{\gamma}
and the nonlinear term
\Gamma_{L}^{\gamma}
are defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{L}^{\gamma}(g,h):=\mu^{-1/2}Q_{L}^{\gamma}(\mu^{1/2}g,\mu^{1/2}h),\quad\mathcal{L}_{L}^{\gamma}g:=-\Gamma_{L}^{\gamma}(\mu^{1/2},g)-\Gamma_{L}^{\gamma}(g,\mu^{1/2}).

(1.21)

Note that the conservation laws imply that for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}F(t,x,v)\phi(v)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}F(0,x,v)\phi(v)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v,\quad\phi(v)=1,v_{j},|v|^{2},\quad j=1,2,3.

(1.22)

Up to suitable choice of the physical parameters in the equilibrium state, without loss of generality, we
assume the initial data satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\sqrt{\mu}f_{0}\phi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v=0,\quad\phi(v)=1,v_{j},|v|^{2},\quad j=1,2,3,\quad F_{0}=\mu+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{0}\geq 0.

(1.23)

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\sqrt{\mu}f(t)\phi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v=0,\quad\phi(v)=1,v_{j},|v|^{2},\quad j=1,2,3.

(1.24)

The case for hard potential with
\gamma+2s\geq 0
is relatively easy because the linearized Boltzmann operator has a spectrum gap. This corresponds to the region
above the blue line in Figure
2
.
Therefore, we only consider the soft potentials in this paper when

\displaystyle 0<s<1,\quad-3<\gamma+2s\leq 0.

(1.25)

Note that this corresponds to the region inside the parallelogram in Figure
2
.
To overcome the lack of spectrum gap, the following weighted energy space is introduced by Guo for the global well-posedness

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{N,l}^{s,\gamma}(f):=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\|f\|^{2}_{H^{N-j}_{x}\dot{H}^{j}_{l+j(\gamma+2s)}}.

(1.26)

If
s=1
, we sometimes write
\mathcal{E}_{N,l}^{\gamma}(f)=\mathcal{E}_{N,l}^{1,\gamma}(f)
which is the functional space for the Landau equation, cf. Subsection
1.3
.

There are three main results given in the following theorem. The first one is the global well-posedness of the Boltzmann equation (
1.18
) in the
parameter range (
1.25
). The second one is the global well-posedness of the Landau equation (
1.20
) for
\gamma>-5
. The last one is about the grazing
limit of the Boltzmann equation (
1.18
) to the Landau equation (
1.20
) by proving a
global-in-time asymptotic formula for the limit
s\to 1^{-}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

[Well-posedness of the Boltzmann equation]

Let
0<s<1,-3<\gamma+2s\leq 0
. Let
N\geq 4,l\geq-N(\gamma+2s)
.
There is a constant
\delta_{s,\gamma,N,l}>0

such that, if

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{s,\gamma}_{N,l}(f_{0})\leq\delta_{s,\gamma,N,l},

(1.27)

then (
1.18
) admits a unique global solution
f^{s,\gamma}
satisfying
\mu+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{s,\gamma}\geq 0
and

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{E}^{s,\gamma}_{N,l}(f^{s,\gamma}({t}))\leq Z_{s,\gamma,N,l}\mathcal{E}^{s,\gamma}_{N,l}(f_{0}),

(1.28)

for some constant
Z_{s,\gamma,N,l}
. Here
\delta_{s,\gamma,N,l}=\frac{1}{2}\eta_{s,\gamma,N,l}^{2}
where

\eta_{s,\gamma,N,l}
is given in Theorem

6.1
.
See (
6.9
) for the definition of
Z_{s,\gamma,N,l}
.
For any fixed
N,l
, there are two functions

\delta_{N,l},Z_{N,l}:(0,1]\times(0,3]\to(0,\infty)

satisfying

\displaystyle\delta_{s,\gamma,N,l}=\delta_{N,l}(s,\gamma+2s+3),\quad Z_{s,\gamma,N,l}=Z_{N,l}(s,\gamma+2s+3),

(1.29)

and

\displaystyle\delta_{N,l}(x_{1},x_{2})\text{ is non-decreasing w.r.t. each argument, and vanishes as }x_{1}\to 0^{+}\text{ or }x_{2}\to 0^{+},

(1.30)

\displaystyle Z_{N,l}(x_{1},x_{2})\text{ is non-increasing w.r.t. each argument and tends to infinity as }x_{1}\to 0^{+}\text{ or }x_{2}\to 0^{+}.

(1.31)

[Well-posedness of the Landau equation]
Taking
s=1
in the above, the global well-posedness of
(
1.20
) holds true. We state this result in details for later discussion.
Let
-3<\gamma+2<0
. Let
N\geq 4,l\geq-N(\gamma+2)
.
Suppose
f_{0}
verify (
1.23
). Let
\delta_{\gamma,N,l}=\delta_{1,\gamma,N,l},Z_{\gamma,N,l}=Z_{1,\gamma,N,l}
.
If

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\gamma}_{N,l}(f_{0})\leq\delta_{\gamma,N,l},

(1.32)

then (
1.20
) admits a unique global solution
f^{\gamma}
satisfying
\mu+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\gamma}\geq 0
and

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{E}^{\gamma}_{N,l}(f^{\gamma}({t}))\leq Z_{\gamma,N,l}\mathcal{E}^{\gamma}_{N,l}(f_{0}).

(1.33)

[Asymptotic formula of the grazing limit]

Fix
-5<\gamma<-2
. Let
N\geq 4,l\geq-N(\gamma+2)
.
Let
s_{*}:=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{\gamma+3}{2})
.
Assume

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\gamma}_{N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5}(f_{0})\leq\delta_{s_{*},\gamma,N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5}.

(1.34)

Since for any
s_{*}\leq s\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{s,\gamma}_{N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5}(f_{0})\leq\mathcal{E}^{\gamma}_{N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5}(f_{0})\leq\delta_{s_{*},\gamma,N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5}\leq\delta_{s,\gamma,N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5},

then
by the above well-posedness result, (
1.18
) has a unique solution
f^{s,\gamma}
for

s_{*}\leq s<1
, and (
1.20
) has a unique solution
f^{\gamma}
.
Moreover,
the family of solutions
\{f^{s,\gamma}\}_{s_{*}\leq s<1}
satisfy

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{E}^{\gamma}_{N,l}(f^{s,\gamma}(t)-f^{\gamma}(t))\leq(1-s)^{2}Z_{s_{*},\gamma,N,l}\exp\left(C_{N,l}Z_{s_{*},\gamma,N,l}^{3}C_{\gamma}^{2}\mathcal{E}_{N+3,l+2N-3\gamma+5}^{1,\gamma}(f_{0})\right).

(1.35)

In the following we will give some remarks on the above result. First of all,
we will keep track of the dependence on the parameters
s,\gamma
. This kind
of dependence gives the precise condition on the parameters for the global well-posedness theory of the Boltzmann and Landau equation. In particular, we have the explicit relation between
\delta,Z
and
s,\gamma

in (
1.29
). On one hand, the region of parameters
for the well-posedness is non-empty as long as
0<s<1,\gamma>-2s-3
. On the other hand, according to (
1.30
),
the region indicated by
the ”radius”
\delta_{s,\gamma,N,l}>0

may shrink
as
s\to 0^{+}
or
\gamma\to(-2s-3)^{+}
.

The estimate (
1.35
) implies that

\displaystyle F^{s,\gamma}_{B}=F^{\gamma}_{L}+(1-s)F_{R}^{s,\gamma},

(1.36)

where
F^{s,\gamma}_{B}
and
F^{\gamma}_{L}
are solutions to (
1.13
) and (
1.1
) respectively. Here the error term
F_{R}^{s,\gamma}
is uniformly bounded in some function space. The formula validates our approach shown in Figure
1
.

A few more remarks are given as follows.

Remark 1.1
.

If
s
and
\gamma
are regarded as two
independent parameters, the well-posedness of the Boltzmann equation holds when
\gamma>-3-2s
and
0<s<1
. In fact, the angular singularity
s\to 1^{-}
is the essential reason
for being possible ill-posedness.

Remark 1.2
.

The global well-posedness in the lower regularity function spaces, such as the one introduced in
[
13
]
and time decay rates as
in
[
27
,
28
,
13
]
for different
non-optimal ranges of the parameters
\gamma
and
s
can be obtained for the parameters in the
optimal range as in Theorem
1.1
. For brevity and to focus on the
key points in this paper, we will not pursue these analysis here.

Remark 1.3
.

In the previous
studies, the reason that the condition

\gamma>-3
is needed is because the following estimate is used,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mu(v_{*})|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\mathrm{d}v_{*}\leq C_{\gamma}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma},

(1.37)

where
C_{\gamma}
is a constant for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
To understand why
\gamma>-3-2s
is sufficient for well-posedness, intuitively, one can
note the
angular singularity in the cross-section leads to a fractional derivative of order
2s
.
Thus, we
can expect to recover of an extra
2s
in the range for
\gamma
below
\gamma=-3
by
sacrificing some regularity of the solution under consideration.

1.3.
Notations

We list some notations that are used in the paper.

Common notations.

We denote the multi-index
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3})
with

|\beta|=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}
.
a\lesssim b
means that there is a
uniform constant
C,
which may be different on different lines,
such that
a\leq Cb
. We use the notation
a\sim b
when
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
. The bracket
\langle\cdot\rangle

is defined by
\langle\cdot\rangle:=(1+|\cdot|^{2})^{1/2}
.
The weight function
W_{l}
is defined by
W_{l}(v):=\langle v\rangle^{l}
.
We denote
C(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{n})
or
C_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{n}}
by a constant depending on parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{n}
.
The notations
\langle f,g\rangle:=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}f(v)g(v)\mathrm{d}v
and
(f,g):=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}\times\mathop{\mathbb{T}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}fg\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v

are used to denote the inner products for the
v
variable and for the
x,v
variables respectively.
As usual,
\mathrm{1}_{A}
is the characteristic function of the set
A
. If
A,B
are two operators, then
[A,B]:=AB-BA
.

Function spaces.
For simplicity, set
\partial^{\alpha}:=\partial^{\alpha}_{x},\partial_{\beta}:=\partial^{\beta}_{v},\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}:=\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{v}
. We will use the following
function spaces.

•

For real number
n,l
,

H^{n}_{l}:=\bigg\{f(v)\big||f|^{2}_{H^{n}_{l}}:=|\langle D\rangle^{n}W_{l}f|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}|(\langle D\rangle^{n}W_{l}f)(v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}v<+\infty\bigg\}.

Here
a(D)
is a differential operator with the symbol

a(\xi)
defined by

\displaystyle\big(a(D)f\big)(v):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}e^{i(v-y)\xi}a(\xi)f(y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi.

•

For
n\in\mathop{\mathbb{N}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits,l\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
,

\displaystyle H^{n}_{l}:=\bigg\{f(v)\big||f|^{2}_{H^{n}_{l}}:=\sum_{|\beta|\leq n}|\partial_{\beta}f|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l}}<\infty\bigg\},

where
|f|_{L^{2}_{l}}:=|W_{l}f|_{L^{2}}
is the usual
L^{2}
norm with weight
W_{l}
.

•

For
n\in\mathop{\mathbb{N}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits,l\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
,

\displaystyle\dot{H}^{n}_{l}:=\bigg\{f(v)\big||f|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{n}_{l}}:=\sum_{|\beta|=n}|\partial_{\beta}f|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l}}<\infty\bigg\}.

(1.38)

•

For
m\in\mathop{\mathbb{N}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
,

H^{m}_{x}:=\bigg\{f(x)\big||f|^{2}_{H^{m}_{x}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}|\partial^{\alpha}f|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}<\infty\bigg\}.

•

For
m,n\in\mathop{\mathbb{N}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits,l\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
,

\displaystyle H^{m}_{x}H^{n}_{l}:=\bigg\{f(x,v)\big|\|f\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{x}H^{n}_{l}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m,|\beta|\leq n}||\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f|_{L^{2}_{l}}|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}<\infty\bigg\}.

We write
\|f\|_{H^{m}_{x}L^{2}_{l}}:=\|f\|_{H^{m}_{x}L^{2}_{l}}
if
n=0
and
\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}L^{2}_{l}}:=\|f\|_{H^{0}_{x}H^{0}_{l}}
if
m=n=0
. The space
H^{m}_{x}\dot{H}^{n}_{l}
can be defined similarly.

Finally in the introduction, let us recall the dissipation norm of the linearized operators
\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{B}
and

\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}_{L}
. More precisely, for
l\in\mathbb{R}
, set

\displaystyle|f|^{2}_{s,l}:=|W_{s}((-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{1/2})W_{l}f|^{2}_{L^{2}}+|W_{s}(D)W_{l}f|^{2}_{L^{2}}+|W_{s}W_{l}f|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

(1.39)

Here
W_{s}(D)
is the pseudo-differential operator with symbol
W_{s}
.
The operator
W_{s}((-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{1/2})
is defined as follows. If
v=r\sigma,r\geq 0,\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, then

\displaystyle(W_{s}((-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{1/2})f)(v):=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}(1+l(l+1))^{\frac{s}{2}}Y^{m}_{l}(\sigma)f^{m}_{l}(r),

(1.40)

where

f^{m}_{l}(r)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}Y^{m}_{l}(\sigma)f(r\sigma)\mathrm{d}\sigma
,
and
Y_{l}^{m},-l\leq m\leq l
are the real spherical harmonics satisfying

(-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})Y_{l}^{m}=l(l+1)Y_{l}^{m}.

Note that the function
W_{s}
is the common weight gain in the three individual norms.
The dissipation norm
|\cdot|_{s,\gamma/2}
characterizes
\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{B}
, see Proposition
3.8
and
Theorem

4.2
for details. Similarly, the dissipation norm
|\cdot|_{1,\gamma/2}
characterizes the
\mathcal{L}^{\gamma}_{L}
.

From time to time, we also write
|f|_{L^{2}_{s,l}}=|f|_{s,l}
. For functions defined on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}
, the space
H^{m}_{x}H^{n}_{s,l}
with
m,n\in\mathop{\mathbb{N}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
is defined by

\displaystyle H^{m}_{x}H^{n}_{s,l}:=\bigg\{f(x,v)\big|\|f\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{x}H^{n}_{s,l}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m,|\beta|\leq n}||\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f|_{L^{2}_{s,l}}|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}<\infty\bigg\}.

Set
\|f\|_{H_{x}^{m}L^{2}_{s,l}}:=\|f\|_{H^{m}_{x}H^{0}_{s,l}}
if
n=0
and
\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}L^{2}_{s,l}}:=\|f\|_{H^{0}_{x}H^{0}_{s,l}}
if
m=n=0
. Again, the space
H^{m}_{x}\dot{H}^{n}_{s,l}
can be defined accordingly.

We sometimes omit the range of some frequently used variables in the integrals.
Usually,
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{2},v,v_{*},u,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
For example,
\int(\cdots)\mathrm{d}\sigma:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdots)\mathrm{d}\sigma,\int(\cdots)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\cdots)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}
.
Integration w.r.t. other variables is understood in a similar way.
Whenever a new variable appears, we will specify its range.

When there is no confusion, we drop the subscripts
B
and
L
in the Boltzmann and Landau operators for brevity.

1.4.
Organization of the paper

In Section
2
, we derive the
upper bound estimates of operators in the singular region. Section
3
contains the upper bound estimates in the regular region. We
will show
the coercivity estimate in Section
4
. The commutator estimates and weighted upper bound estimates are given in Section
5
. The proof Theorem

1.1
is given in Section
6
. In the Appendix
7
, for completeness,
we prove the operator convergence stated in Proposition
6.2
.

2.
Upper bound estimate in the singular region

In this section, we will derive the upper bound estimates on the collision operators in the singular region

|v-v_{*}|\lesssim\eta
. For this, we first recall the dyadic decomposition.

2.1.
Dyadic decomposition

Let
\varphi
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying

\displaystyle\varphi=0\text{ on }[0,3/4],\quad\varphi\text{ is strictly increasing on }[3/4,4/3],\quad\varphi=1\text{ on }[4/3,3/2],

\displaystyle\varphi\text{ is strictly decreasing on }[3/2,8/3],\quad\varphi=0\text{ on }[8/3,\infty),\quad|\varphi^{\prime}|\leq 4.

Moreover,
\varphi
is chosen such that
the functions
\{\varphi_{k}(\cdot)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi(\cdot/2^{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a partition of unit on
(0,\infty)
. That is,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\varphi_{k}=1\text{ on }(0,\infty).

(2.1)

Set
\psi(r)=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{-1}\varphi_{k}(r)
for
r>0
and
\psi(0)=1
. Then
\psi
is a non-increasing smooth function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\psi=1\text{ on }[0,3/4],\quad\psi\text{ is strictly decreasing on }[3/4,4/3],\quad\psi=0\text{ on }[4/3,\infty),\quad|\psi^{\prime}|\leq 4.

(2.2)

Then we have the identity

\displaystyle\psi+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{k}\equiv 1\text{ on }[0,\infty).

(2.3)

With a little abuse of notation, we define radial functions
\varphi(v)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi(|v|),\psi(v)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\psi(|v|),\varphi_{k}(v)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi_{k}(|v|)
for
v\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}
.

Given a general Boltzmann kernel
B=B(v-v_{*},\sigma)=B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)
where
\cos\theta=\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\cdot\sigma
, let
Q
be the Boltzmann operator with kernel
B
. That is,

\displaystyle Q(g,h)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\cdot\sigma)(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(2.4)

Let
\mathcal{U}_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{j\leq k}\varphi_{j},\tilde{\varphi}_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{|j-k|\leq N_{0}-1}\varphi_{j}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
for some fixed integer
N_{0}\geq 4
.
Suppose the relative velocity satisfies
|v-v_{*}|\leq\frac{4}{3}
. Let
v_{*}\in\{\frac{3}{4}\times 2^{j}\leq|v_{*}|\leq\frac{8}{3}\times 2^{j}\}
. Then the fact that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}|v-v_{*}|\leq|v^{\prime}-v_{*}|\leq|v-v_{*}|,

yields

•

If
j\geq N_{0}-1
, then
|v|,|v^{\prime}|\in[(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{8}{3}\times 2^{-N_{0}})2^{j},\frac{8}{3}(1+2^{-N_{0}})2^{j}]\subset\mathrm{Supp}\tilde{\varphi}_{j}
.

•

If
j\leq N_{0}-2
, then
|v|,|v^{\prime}|\leq\frac{3}{2}\times 2^{N_{0}-1}\subset\mathrm{Supp}\mathcal{U}_{N_{0}-1}
.

Hence, if
Q
is localized in
|v-v_{*}|\leq\frac{4}{3}
, then

\displaystyle\langle Q(g,h),f\rangle=\sum_{j\geq N_{0}-1}\langle Q(\varphi_{j}g,\tilde{\varphi}_{j}h),\tilde{\varphi}_{j}f\rangle+\langle Q(\mathcal{U}_{N_{0}-2}g,\mathcal{U}_{N_{0}-1}h),\mathcal{U}_{N_{0}-1}f\rangle.

(2.5)

Let us recall the Bobylev formula which is about the Fourier transform of the Boltzmann operator. By (
2.4
), the Bobylev formula reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(Q(g,h))(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big(\hat{B}(|\eta_{*}-\xi^{-}|,\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)-\hat{B}(|\eta_{*}|,\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)\big)\mathcal{F}g(\eta_{*})\mathcal{F}h(\xi-\eta_{*})\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\eta_{*},

where

\displaystyle\hat{B}(|\xi|,\cos\theta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}B(|q|,\cos\theta)e^{-iq\cdot\xi}\mathrm{d}q.

Here
\mathcal{F}
is the Fourier transform operator. As usual, denote
\hat{f}=\mathcal{F}f
, then

\displaystyle\langle Q(g,h),f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{Q(g,h)},\hat{f}\rangle

(2.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big(\hat{B}(|\eta_{*}-\xi^{-}|,\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)-\hat{B}(|\eta_{*}|,\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)\big)\hat{g}(\eta_{*})\hat{h}(\xi-\eta_{*})\bar{\hat{f}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\eta_{*}\mathrm{d}\xi.

Note that

\displaystyle||\xi|-|\eta_{*}||\leq|\xi-\eta_{*}|\leq|\xi|+|\eta_{*}|.

Fix
j,p\in\mathbb{Z}
, suppose
\frac{3}{4}\times 2^{p}\leq|\eta_{*}|\leq\frac{8}{3}\times 2^{p}
and
\frac{3}{4}\times 2^{j}\leq|\xi-\eta_{*}|\leq\frac{8}{3}\times 2^{j}
. Since
N_{0}\geq 4
, then

•

If
p\leq j-N_{0}
, then
|\xi|\in[(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{8}{3}\times 2^{-N_{0}})2^{j},\frac{8}{3}(1+2^{-N_{0}})2^{j}]\subset\mathrm{Supp}\tilde{\varphi}_{j}
.

•

If
p\geq j+N_{0}
, then
|\xi|\in[(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{8}{3}\times 2^{-N_{0}})2^{p},\frac{8}{3}(1+2^{-N_{0}})2^{p}]\subset\mathrm{Supp}\tilde{\varphi}_{p}
.

•

If
|p-j|<N_{0}
, then
|\xi|\in[0,\frac{3}{2}\times 2^{p+N_{0}}]\cap[0,\frac{3}{2}\times 2^{j+N_{0}}]\subset\mathrm{Supp}\mathcal{U}_{p+N_{0}}\cap\mathrm{Supp}\mathcal{U}_{j+N_{0}}
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{-1}f(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\psi(D)f,\quad\mathfrak{F}_{j}f(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi_{j}(D)f,j\geq 0.

(2.7)

\mathfrak{F}_{-1}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{j}
localize the frequency of function
f
in the region
|\xi|\lesssim 1
and
|\xi|\sim 2^{j}
respectively. By (
2.3
),
the dyadic decomposition in frequency space reads

\displaystyle f=\sum_{j\geq-1}\mathfrak{F}_{j}f.

Note that
\mathfrak{F}_{-1}
has symbol
\psi
instead of
\varphi_{-1}
. Set

\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}:=\tilde{\varphi}_{k}(D)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\langle Q(g,h),f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq N_{0}-1}\sum_{-1\leq p\leq j-N_{0}}\langle Q(\mathfrak{F}_{p}g,\mathfrak{F}_{j}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle+\sum_{p\geq N_{0}-1}\sum_{-1\leq j\leq p-N_{0}}\langle Q(\mathfrak{F}_{p}g,\mathfrak{F}_{j}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{p}f\rangle

(2.8)

\displaystyle+\sum_{p,j\geq-1,|p-j|<N_{0}}\sum_{q\leq j+N_{0}}\langle Q(\mathfrak{F}_{p}g,\mathfrak{F}_{j}h),\mathfrak{F}_{q}f\rangle.

For completeness, we recall the definition of symbol class
S^{m}_{1,0}
as follows.

Definition 2.1
.

A smooth function
W(v,\xi)
is said to be a symbol of type
S^{m}_{1,0}
if for any multi-indices
\alpha
and
\beta
,

\displaystyle|(\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}\partial^{\beta}_{v}W)(v,\xi)|\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}\langle\xi\rangle^{m-|\alpha|},

where
C_{\alpha,\beta}
is a constant depending only on
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
l,m,r\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits,M(v,\xi)=M(\xi)\in S^{r}_{1,0}
and
\Phi(v,\xi)=\Phi(\xi)\in S^{l}_{1,0}
. Then there exists a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle|[M(D),\Phi]f|_{H^{m}}\leq C|f|_{H^{r+s-1}_{l-1}}.

See Lemma 5.3 in
[
22
]
for the proof of Lemma
2.1
.
Based on Lemma
2.1
, one directly has

\displaystyle|f|_{H^{m}_{l}}^{2}\sim\sum_{j,k=-1}^{\infty}2^{2mk}2^{2lj}|\varphi_{k}(D)\varphi_{j}f|_{L^{2}}^{2}\sim\sum_{j,k=-1}^{\infty}2^{2mk}2^{2lj}|\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}(D)f|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(2.9)

Here for simplicity, we take
\varphi_{-1}:=\psi
. By (
2.9
), we use both
|W_{l}f|_{H^{m+s}}
and
|W_{l}W_{s}(D)f|_{H^{m}}
in the rest of the paper.

2.2.
Operator splitting

We divide the relative velocity into two parts

\displaystyle B(v-v_{*},\sigma)=B_{\eta}^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)+B^{s,\gamma,\eta}(v-v_{*},\sigma),

where

\displaystyle B_{\eta}^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma):=\psi_{\eta}(|v-v_{*}|)B(v-v_{*},\sigma),\quad B^{s,\gamma,\eta}(v-v_{*},\sigma):=(1-\psi_{\eta}(|v-v_{*}|))B(v-v_{*},\sigma).

(2.10)

Note that
B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
is supported in
|v-v_{*}|\leq 4\eta/3
so that it is singular when
|v-v_{*}|\to 0
, while
B^{s,\gamma,\eta}
is supported in
|v-v_{*}|\geq 3\eta/4

without singularity.

Let
Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
and
Q^{s,\gamma,\eta}
be the Boltzmann operators defined with kernel

B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
and
B^{s,\gamma,\eta}
respectively.
And then let
\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
and
\Gamma^{s,\gamma,\eta}
be the nonlinear terms defined with kernel

B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
and
B^{s,\gamma,\eta}
respectively.

Recall that the nonlinear term
\Gamma
for a general kernel
B
is defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-1/2}Q(\mu^{1/2}g,\mu^{1/2}h)=\int B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\mu^{1/2}_{*}\big(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h\big)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\big((\mu^{1/2}g)^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-(\mu^{1/2}g)_{*}h\big)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}

\displaystyle+\int B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\big(\mu^{1/2}_{*}-(\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}\big)g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}=Q(\mu^{1/2}g,h)+I(g,h),

where for brevity, we define

\displaystyle I(g,h):=\int B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\big(\mu^{1/2}_{*}-(\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}\big)g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(2.11)

Let
I^{s,\gamma},I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
and
I^{s,\gamma,\eta}
be the bi-linear operators defined according to (
2.11
) with kernel

B^{s,\gamma},B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
and
B^{s,\gamma,\eta}
respectively. Other operators
with such subscripts and superscripts are understood in the same way.
With these notations, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma}(\mu^{1/2}g,h)+I^{s,\gamma}(g,h),

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma,\eta}(\mu^{1/2}g,h)+I^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}g,h)+I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h),

(2.14)

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h)+Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h),

(2.15)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h)+\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h),

(2.16)

\displaystyle I^{s,\gamma}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h)+I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h).

(2.17)

To implement the energy estimates for the nonlinear equations, we need to take derivatives.
By binomial expansion, we have

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(g,h)=\sum_{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta,\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=\alpha}C^{\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}_{\beta}C^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}_{\alpha}\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{\beta_{1}}g,\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{\beta_{2}}h;\beta_{0}),

(2.18)

where

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(g,h;\beta)(v):=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{S}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{2}\times\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)(\partial_{\beta}\mu^{1/2})_{*}(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(2.19)

Note that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(g,h;\beta)=Q^{s,\gamma}(g\partial_{\beta}\mu^{1/2},h)+I^{s,\gamma}(g,h;\beta),

(2.20)

where

\displaystyle I^{s,\gamma}(g,h;\beta):=\int B^{s,\gamma}(v-v_{*},\sigma)\big((\partial_{\beta}\mu^{1/2})_{*}-(\partial_{\beta}\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}\big)g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

(2.21)

Thus, in general we need to consider
I^{s,\gamma}(g,h;\beta)
. This is again divided into two parts:

I^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h;\beta)
and
I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta)
.

Recall

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}f=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}},f)-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(f,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}).

By binomial expansion, we have

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}f=\sum_{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta}C^{\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}_{\beta}\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta_{2}}f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),

(2.22)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}):=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(\partial_{\beta_{1}}\mu^{1/2},f;\beta_{0})-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(f,\partial_{\beta_{1}}\mu^{1/2};\beta_{0}).

(2.23)

We also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{1}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}):=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(\partial_{\beta_{1}}\mu^{1/2},f;\beta_{0}),\quad\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{2}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}):=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}(f,\partial_{\beta_{1}}\mu^{1/2};\beta_{0}).

(2.24)

In the same way, we can define

\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma,\eta}(\cdot;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma,\eta}_{1}(\cdot;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma,\eta}_{2}(\cdot;\beta_{0},\beta_{1})
with kernel
B^{s,\gamma,\eta}

and
\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\cdot;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,1}(\cdot;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}(\cdot;\beta_{0},\beta_{1})
with kernel
B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
.

2.3.
Taylor expansion and symmetry

When evaluating the difference
f^{\prime}-f
(or
f^{\prime}_{*}-f_{*}
) before and after collision, Taylor expansion is applied. We first denote the 1st-order expansion by

\displaystyle f^{\prime}-f=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla f)(v(\kappa))\cdot(v^{\prime}-v)\mathrm{d}\kappa,\quad f^{\prime}_{*}-f_{*}=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla f)(v_{*}(\iota))\cdot(v^{\prime}_{*}-v_{*})\mathrm{d}\iota.

(2.25)

To cancel the angular singularity, the second order expansion is needed:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}-f=(\nabla f)(v)\cdot(v^{\prime}-v)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-\kappa)(\nabla^{2}f)(v(\kappa)):(v^{\prime}-v)\otimes(v^{\prime}-v)\mathrm{d}\kappa,

(2.26)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}-f=(\nabla f)(v^{\prime})\cdot(v^{\prime}-v)-\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(\nabla^{2}f)(v(\kappa)):(v^{\prime}-v)\otimes(v^{\prime}-v)\mathrm{d}\kappa.

(2.27)

Thanks to the symmetry property of
\sigma
-integral, we have

\displaystyle\int B(|v-v_{*}|,\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\cdot\sigma)(v^{\prime}-v)\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int B(|v-v_{*}|,\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\cdot\sigma)\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}(v_{*}-v)\mathrm{d}\sigma,

(2.28)

\displaystyle\int B(|v-v_{*}|,\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\cdot\sigma)(v^{\prime}-v)h(v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}v=0.

(2.29)

Here, the formula (
2.28
) holds for fixed
v,v_{*}
and (
2.29
) holds for fixed
v_{*}
.

We now recall a useful formula in the following lemma on the change of variables
v\to v(\kappa)
and
v_{*}\to v_{*}(\iota)

where for
\kappa,\iota\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle v(\kappa)=\kappa v^{\prime}+(1-\kappa)v,\quad v_{*}(\iota)=\iota v^{\prime}_{*}+(1-\iota)v_{*}.

(2.30)

Lemma 2.2
.

For
a\in[0,2]
, let us define

\displaystyle\psi_{a}(\theta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\cos^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}+(1-a)^{2}\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2})^{-1/2}.

(2.31)

For any
0\leq\kappa,\iota\leq 1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{S}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{2}_{+}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)g(\iota(v_{*}))f(v(\kappa))\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}\sigma

(2.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{S}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{2}_{+}}B(|v-v_{*}|\psi_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta),\cos\theta)g(v_{*})f(v)\psi_{\kappa+\iota}^{3}(\theta)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}\sigma.

Here
\mathop{\mathbb{S}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{2}_{+}
stands for
(v-v_{*})\cdot\sigma\geq 0
.

Before giving the proof of this lemma, we firstly note that the above formula is general as it simultaneously deals with the two changes
v\to v(\kappa)
and
v_{*}\to v_{*}(\iota)
. This will be used in the proof of Proposition
6.2
.
If
\kappa=\iota=0
, then
\psi_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta)=\psi_{0}(\theta)=1

and it corresponds to the identity transformation. If
\kappa=\iota=1
, then
\psi_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta)=\psi_{2}(\theta)=1

and it corresponds to the change of velocities for pre-post collision:
(v,v_{*},\sigma)\rightarrow(v^{\prime},v_{*}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}=(v-v_{*})/|v-v_{*}|)
. If
\kappa=1,\iota=0
or
\kappa=0,\iota=1
, then
\psi_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta)=\psi_{1}(\theta)=\cos^{-1}\frac{\theta}{2}

and it corresponds to
v\to v^{\prime}
or
v_{*}\to v^{\prime}_{*}

respectively. This is consistent with the cancellation lemma given in
[
1
]
. If
\iota=0
or
\kappa=0
, then it corresponds to the individual change
v\to v(\kappa)
or
v_{*}\to v_{*}(\iota)
respectively.

Note that
1\leq\psi_{a}(\theta)\leq\sqrt{2}

for
a\in[0,2],\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
. Thanks to Lemma
2.2
and
1\leq\psi_{a}(\theta)\leq\sqrt{2}
, considering the kernel (
1.16
),
we can skip the details regarding the above mentioned change of variables. As a result,
in most part of this paper,

v(\kappa)
and
v_{*}(\iota)
will be replaced by
v
and
v_{*}
respectively at the cost of a multiplicative constant.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

The case
\kappa=\iota=1
is obviously given by the standard change of variable
(v,v_{*},\sigma)\to(v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{*},\sigma^{\prime})
where
\sigma^{\prime}=(v-v_{*})/|v-v_{*}|
. This change has unit Jacobian.

Now we deal with the case
\kappa+\iota<2
. Recalling (
2.30
), it is direct to check

\displaystyle|v-v_{*}|=|\kappa(v)-\iota(v_{*})|\psi_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta).

(2.33)

Let
\beta
be the angle between
\kappa(v)-\iota(v_{*})
and
\sigma
, then
\cos\beta=\varphi_{\kappa+\iota}(\sin\frac{\theta}{2})
where

\displaystyle\varphi_{a}(x):=\frac{1-x^{2}+(a-1)x^{2}}{\left(1-x^{2}+(1-a)^{2}x^{2}\right)^{1/2}}.

Let
\delta_{a}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\arccos(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+(1-a)^{2}}})
.
If
\kappa+\iota<2
, then
\delta_{\kappa+\iota}>0

and
the function:

\theta\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]\to\beta_{\kappa+\iota}\in[0,\delta_{\kappa+\iota}]

is a bijection. It holds that

\displaystyle\det(\frac{\partial(\kappa(v),\iota(v_{*}))}{\partial(v,v_{*})})=\alpha_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta),

(2.34)

where for
0\leq a\leq 2
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{a}(\theta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(1-\frac{a}{2})^{2}\left((1-\frac{a}{2})+\frac{a}{2}\cos\theta\right).

(2.35)

By (
2.33
) and (
2.34
), with
\mathrm{d}\sigma=\sin\beta\mathrm{d}\beta\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{S}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{2}_{+}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)g(\iota(v_{*}))f(v(\kappa))\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}}\int_{0}^{\delta_{\kappa+\iota}}B(|v-v_{*}|\psi_{\kappa+\iota}(\theta),\cos\theta)g(v_{*})f(v)\alpha_{\kappa+\iota}^{-1}(\theta)\sin\beta\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}\beta.

It is directly to check that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\kappa+\iota}^{-1}(\theta)\sin\beta\mathrm{d}\beta=-\alpha_{\kappa+\iota}^{-1}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\cos\beta=-\frac{1}{4}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa+\iota}(\sin\frac{\theta}{2})\sin^{-1}\frac{\theta}{2}\alpha_{\kappa+\iota}^{-1}(\theta)\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta=\psi_{\kappa+\iota}^{3}(\theta)\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta.

Then we go back from
\beta
to
\theta
and use
\mathrm{d}\sigma=\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}

to get (
2.32
).
∎

2.4.
Upper bound of
Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}

We give the upper bound of
Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
0<s<1,-2s-3<\gamma\leq 0,0<\eta\leq 1
. Let
l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
satisfying
l_{1}+l_{2}+l_{3}=0
.
For any fixed small
1/2\geq\delta>0
, for any combination
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\geq 0,a_{1}+a_{2}\geq s,a_{1}+a_{3}\geq 2s,a_{2}+a_{3}\geq 2s
satisfying the constraint

a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=2s+\frac{3}{2}+\delta
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h),f\rangle|\lesssim C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}_{l_{1}}}|h|_{H^{a_{2}}_{l_{2}}}|f|_{H^{a_{3}}_{l_{3}}},

where

\displaystyle C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}:=\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}C_{s,\gamma,\eta},\quad C_{s,\gamma,\eta}:=\frac{1}{s}\frac{\eta^{\gamma+2s+3}}{\gamma+2s+3}.

(2.36)

We point out that the constant associated to
\lesssim
in the above inequality
depends only on the upper bound of
|l_{1}|,|l_{2}|,|l_{3}|
.

Proof.

Recalling the decomposition in frequency space, we have

\displaystyle\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h),f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\leq k-N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f\rangle+\sum_{|j-k|<N_{0}}\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\geq k+N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle.

We estimate the second quantity in details for illustration. That is, when
|j-k|<N_{0},l\leq k+N_{0}
. Note that

\displaystyle\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}\mathfrak{F}_{k}h((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V,

where for brevity of notiation,
\mathrm{d}V=\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}\sigma
.
Motivated by (
2.29
), we write

\displaystyle\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime}((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle+\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h-(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime})((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V:=\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}.

For
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
let
E:=\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\leq 2^{-l}|v-v_{*}|^{-1}\wedge\sqrt{2}/2
and
write

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{1,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)+\mathcal{I}_{1,\geq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f):=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mathrm{1}_{E}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime}((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,\geq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f):=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mathrm{1}_{E^{c}}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime}((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V.

For term
\mathcal{I}_{1,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)
, we apply
(
2.27
) and (
2.29
) to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mathrm{1}_{E}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)(v(\kappa)):(v^{\prime}-v)\otimes(v^{\prime}-v)\mathrm{d}\kappa\right)\mathrm{d}V.

By using
|\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{7}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}
, the change of variable
v\to v(\kappa)
, and
\int\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}b^{s}(\theta)\mathrm{1}_{E}\mathrm{d}\sigma\lesssim 2^{2sl-2l}|v-v_{*}|^{2s-2}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)|\lesssim 2^{2sl}2^{\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}\int|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma+2s}\mathrm{1}_{|v-v_{*}|\leq 4\eta/3}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

By using the fact that

\displaystyle||\cdot|^{\gamma+2s}\mathrm{1}_{|\cdot|\leq 4\eta/3}|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+2s+3}}{\gamma+2s+3},

(2.37)

we get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)|\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+2s+3}}{\gamma+2s+3}2^{2sl+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Since
\int b^{s}(\theta)\mathrm{1}_{E^{c}}\mathrm{d}\sigma\lesssim s^{-1}2^{2sl}|v-v_{*}|^{2s}
, then

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1,\geq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sl}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sl+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Combining these estimates on
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sl+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Now we estimate

\mathcal{I}_{2}
. Let
F:=\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\leq 2^{-l/2-k/2}|v-v_{*}|^{-1}\wedge\sqrt{2}/2
.
We write

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{2,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)+\mathcal{I}_{1,\geq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f):=-\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mathrm{1}_{F}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}((\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,\geq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f):=-\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mathrm{1}_{F^{c}}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}((\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)((\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)^{\prime}-\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)\mathrm{d}V.

By the 1st-order Taylor expansion (
2.25
), using
|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{5}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}
, the change of variable in Lemma
2.31
,
and
\int\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}b^{s}(\theta)\mathrm{1}_{F}\mathrm{d}\sigma\lesssim 2^{sl+sk-l-k}|v-v_{*}|^{2s-2}
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)|\lesssim 2^{sl+sk-k}2^{\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}\int|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma+2s}\mathrm{1}_{|v-v_{*}|\leq 4\eta/3}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g)_{*}(\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{k}h)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}v_{*}.

Then (
2.37
) implies

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2,\leq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)|\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+2s+3}}{\gamma+2s+3}2^{sl+sk-k+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+2s+3}}{\gamma+2s+3}2^{sl+sk+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Since
\int b^{s}(\theta)\mathrm{1}_{F^{c}}\mathrm{d}\sigma\lesssim s^{-1}2^{sl+sk}|v-v_{*}|^{2s}
, then

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2,\geq}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h,\mathfrak{F}_{l}f)|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{sl+sk}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{sl+sk+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Combining these estimates on
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{sl+sk+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Therefore, when
l\leq k+N_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{sl+sk+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

(2.38)

Finally, to

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-k|<N_{0}}\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle,

let
a+b=s+\frac{3}{2}
for
a\geq 0
.
For any fixed
k
, the sum over
-1\leq l\leq k+N_{0}
can be estimated by using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality as

\displaystyle\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}2^{sl+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}\leq\left(\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}2^{2al}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}2^{2bl}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim|f|_{H^{a}}C_{b,k},

(2.39)

where
for
b\neq 0
,

\displaystyle C_{b,k}^{2}=\frac{2^{2b(k+N_{0}+1)}-2^{-2b}}{2^{2b}-1}.

For
b
close to
0
, by allowing an extra
\delta
-order regularity,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-k|<N_{0}}\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle\lesssim C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}}|h|_{H^{a_{2}}}|f|_{H^{a_{3}}},

(2.40)

where
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\geq 0,a_{1}+a_{2}\geq s
satisfying the constraint

a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=2s+\frac{3}{2}+\delta
for any fixed small
\delta>0
. Indeed, recalling (
2.39
), in which we can take
a+b=s+\frac{3}{2}+\delta
and
b\geq\frac{\delta}{2}
, then
C_{b,k}\lesssim\delta^{-1/2}2^{k\delta/2}
. Since
|j-k|<N_{0}
,
we get (
2.40
) for
a_{1}+a_{2}\geq s+\frac{\delta}{2}
.
In (
2.39
)
we can also take
=s+\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2},b=-\frac{\delta}{2}
and so
C_{b,k}\lesssim\delta^{-1/2}
, then we get (
2.40
) for
a_{1}+a_{2}=s,a_{3}=s+\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}
. This obviously implies that (
2.40
) holds for
s\leq a_{1}+a_{2}\leq s+\frac{\delta}{2}
.

Similar argument can be applied to
\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f\rangle
for
j\leq k-N_{0}

to obtain

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sk+\frac{3}{2}j}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f|_{L^{2}}.

(2.41)

Here we take
L^{\infty}
on
\mathfrak{F}_{j}g
so that there is a factor
2^{\frac{3}{2}j}
. We also apply Taylor expansions to
\mathfrak{F}_{k}h
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f
to obtain the factor
2^{2sk}
.
Let
0<\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, for any combination
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\geq 0,a_{2}+a_{3}\geq 2s
satisfying the constraint

a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=2s+\frac{3}{2}+\delta
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\sum_{j\leq k-N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f\rangle|\lesssim\delta^{-1/2}C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}}|h|_{H^{a_{2}}}|f|_{H^{a_{3}}}.

Again similar argument can be applied to
\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle
for
j\geq k+N_{0}

to get

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sj+\frac{3}{2}k}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f|_{L^{2}}.

(2.42)

Here we also apply Taylor expansions to
\mathfrak{F}_{k}h
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f
to get the factor
2^{2sj}
or
2^{sk+sj}\leq 2^{2sj}
.
Here we take
L^{\infty}
on
\mathfrak{F}_{k}h
or
\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{k}h
so that
at the end there is a factor
2^{\frac{3}{2}k}
. Let
0<\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, for any combination
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\geq 0,a_{1}+a_{3}\geq 2s
satisfying the constraint

a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=2s+\frac{3}{2}+\delta
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\sum_{j\geq k+N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle|\lesssim\delta^{-1/2}C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}}|h|_{H^{a_{2}}}|f|_{H^{a_{3}}}.

In summary, we prove the desired estimate with
l_{1}=l_{2}=l_{3}=0
.

By (
2.5
) and (
2.9
), we can freely transfer weight among
g,h,f
so that the estimate in the proposition holds for
l_{1}+l_{2}+l_{3}=0
.
∎

Based on the proof of the above proposition, we will derive another
version of cancellation lemma introduced in
[
1
]
.
The idea is to gain
|v-v_{*}|^{2s}
at the price of
2s
-order derivatives on the functions.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
0<s<1,\gamma+2s+3>0
.
Let
a_{1},a_{2},l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits
satisfying

a_{1}+a_{2}=2s,l_{1}+l_{2}=0
, then

\displaystyle|\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}g_{*}(f^{\prime}-f)\mathrm{d}V|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}_{l_{1}}}|f|_{H^{a_{2}}_{l_{2}}}.

Proof.

Recalling the decomposition in frequency space, we have

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}g_{*}(f^{\prime}-f)\mathrm{d}V=\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,1),f\rangle=\sum_{|j-l|<N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,1),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle.

This is because frequency of
g
and
f
lies in the same region when
h=1
.
Following the estimate on
\mathcal{I}_{1}
in the proof of Proposition
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,1),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sl}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Since
|j-l|<N_{0}
, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
we can estimate the sum as

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-l|<N_{0}}2^{2sl}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|g|_{H^{a_{1}}}|f|_{H^{a_{2}}}.

By (
2.5
), we can freely transfer weight among
g,f
so that the proof of the proposition is completed.
∎

2.5.
Upper bound of
\langle I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta),f\rangle

In this subsection, we will estimate the upper bound of
\langle I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta),f\rangle
where
I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta)
is defined by
(
2.21
) with
B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
l\geq 0
. Let
(a_{1},a_{2})=(\frac{3}{2}+\delta,s)
or
(0,\frac{3}{2}+\delta)
. Then it holds

\displaystyle\langle I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta),f\rangle\lesssim_{l}C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}_{-l}}|h|_{H^{a_{2}}_{-l}}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

Proof.

We only need to consider the case when
\beta=0
because
the following argument also holds when we replace
\mu^{1/2}
by
P_{\beta}\mu^{1/2}
.
Recall

\displaystyle\langle I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h),f\rangle=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/2})_{*}^{\prime}-\mu_{*}^{1/2})g_{*}hf^{\prime}\mathrm{d}V=\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2},

(2.43)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}:=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/2})_{*}^{\prime}-\mu_{*}^{1/2})g_{*}h(f^{\prime}-f)\mathrm{d}V,\quad\mathcal{I}_{2}:=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/2})_{*}^{\prime}-\mu_{*}^{1/2})g_{*}hf\mathrm{d}V.

Firstly, for
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\leq\mathcal{I}_{1,1}^{1/2}\mathcal{I}_{1,2}^{1/2}
,
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}:=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/4})_{*}^{\prime}+\mu_{*}^{1/4})^{2}(f^{\prime}-f)^{2}\mathrm{d}V,\quad\mathcal{I}_{1,2}:=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/4})_{*}^{\prime}-\mu_{*}^{1/4})^{2}g_{*}^{2}h^{2}\mathrm{d}V.

Using
((\mu^{1/4})_{*}^{\prime}+\mu_{*}^{1/4})^{2}\leq 2((\mu^{1/2})_{*}^{\prime}+\mu_{*}^{1/2})
, by the change of variable
(v,v_{*},\sigma)\rightarrow(v^{\prime},v_{*}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}\leq 4\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mu_{*}^{1/2}(f^{\prime}-f)^{2}\mathrm{d}V=4\mathcal{N}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/4},f),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h):=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}g^{2}_{*}(h^{\prime}-h)^{2}\mathrm{d}V.

(2.44)

Using
(f^{\prime}-f)^{2}=(f^{2})^{\prime}-f^{2}-2f(f^{\prime}-f)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/4},f)=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mu_{*}^{1/2}((f^{2})^{\prime}-f^{2})\mathrm{d}V-2\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2},f),f\rangle.

(2.45)

By Taylor expansion of
(\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}-(\mu^{1/2})_{*}
, we have

\displaystyle|\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}\mu_{*}^{1/2}((f^{2})^{\prime}-f^{2})\mathrm{d}V|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}-(\mu^{1/2})_{*})f^{2}\mathrm{d}V|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{1}_{|v-v_{*}|\leq 4\eta/3}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma+2}\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}f^{2}\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}v\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+5}}{\gamma+5}|\mu^{\frac{1}{8}}f|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

By Proposition
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2},f),f\rangle|\lesssim_{l}C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}^{2}.

Since
\gamma+5\geq\gamma+2s+3
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/4},f)\lesssim_{l}C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}^{2}.

(2.46)

It is straightforward to have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,2}\lesssim\int\mathrm{1}_{|v-v_{*}|\leq 4\eta/3}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma+2}\mu^{1/8}\mu^{1/8}_{*}g_{*}^{2}h^{2}\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}v\lesssim\delta^{-1}\frac{\eta^{\gamma+5}}{\gamma+5}|\mu^{1/16}g|_{H^{a_{1}}}^{2}|\mu^{1/16}h|_{H^{a_{2}}}^{2},

where
a_{1}+a_{2}=\frac{3}{2}+\delta
.
Combining the estimates on
\mathcal{I}_{1,1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1,2}
gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}\lesssim_{l}C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|\mu^{1/16}g|_{H^{a_{1}}}|\mu^{1/16}h|_{H^{a_{2}}}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

We now turn to estimate
\mathcal{I}_{2}
. By Prop.
2.1
, we directly have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}|=|\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}((\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}-\mu^{1/2})g(hf)_{*}\mathrm{d}V|=|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(hf,g),\mu^{1/2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|hf|_{H^{s}_{-2l}}|g|_{L^{2}_{-l}}|\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}|_{H^{s+2}_{3l}}\lesssim_{l}C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{L^{2}_{-l}}|h|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}_{-l}}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

Similarly, by Lemma
2.3
to be proved later, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}|=|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(hf,g),\mu^{1/2}\rangle|\lesssim_{l}C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}_{-l}}|h|_{H^{s}_{-l}}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

Combining the estimates on
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}

completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

It holds that

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(f_{2}f_{3},f_{1}),\mu^{1/2}\rangle|\lesssim_{l}C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{1}|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}_{-l}}|f_{2}|_{H^{s}_{-l}}|f_{3}|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

Proof.

We will follow the proof of Prop.
2.1
by taking
g=f_{2}f_{3},h=f_{1},f=\mu^{1/2}
.
Recall (
2.38
) for

l\leq k+N_{0}
that

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{sl+sk+\frac{3}{2}l}|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}.

Note that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{F}_{j}g|_{L^{2}}=|\varphi_{j}(\hat{f_{2}}*\hat{f_{3}})|_{L^{2}}\leq|\varphi_{j}|_{L^{r}}|\hat{f_{2}}|_{L^{q}}|\hat{f_{3}}|_{L^{q}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{3}{r}j}|\hat{f_{2}}|_{L^{q}}|\hat{f_{3}}|_{L^{q}}.

(2.47)

Here
r\geq 2,q\leq 2
satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}+\frac{2}{q}=1+\frac{1}{2},\quad\frac{3}{r}=\frac{3}{2}-s.

For the chosen
r
, when
|j-k|<N_{0}
, we have

2^{\frac{3}{r}j}2^{sk}\lesssim 2^{\frac{3}{2}k}
.
For
1/q=1/2+1/p
, we have

\displaystyle|\hat{f_{2}}|_{L^{q}}\leq|W_{s}\hat{f_{2}}|_{L^{2}}|W_{-s}|_{L^{p}}\lesssim|f_{2}|_{H^{s}},

because
sp=6
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{|j-k|<N_{0}}\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\mathfrak{F}_{l}f\rangle\lesssim\sum_{|j-k|<N_{0}}\sum_{l\leq k+N_{0}}C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{sl+\frac{3}{2}l+\frac{3}{2}k}|f_{2}|_{H^{s}}|f_{3}|_{H^{s}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{l}f|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{H^{s}}|f_{3}|_{H^{s}}|f|_{H^{s+2}}\sum_{|j-k|<N_{0}}2^{\frac{3}{2}k}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{H^{s}}|f_{3}|_{H^{s}}|f|_{H^{s+2}}|h|_{H^{3/2+\delta}}\lesssim C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{1}|_{H^{3/2+\delta}}|f_{2}|_{H^{s}}|f_{3}|_{H^{s}}.

Recalling (
2.41
) for
j\leq k-N_{0}
, and using (
2.47
) for
r=q=2
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sk+3j}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f|_{L^{2}},

which yields

\displaystyle|\sum_{j\leq k-N_{0}}\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}\sum_{j\leq k-N_{0}}2^{2sk+3j}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}\sum_{k}2^{2sk+3k}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}|h|_{L^{2}}|f|_{H^{2s+3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{1}|_{L^{2}}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}.

Recalling (
2.42
)
for
j\geq k+N_{0}
, and using (
2.47
) for
r=q=2
,
we have

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}2^{2sj+\frac{3}{2}j+\frac{3}{2}k}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f|_{L^{2}},

which yields

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq k+N_{0}}|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{F}_{j}g,\mathfrak{F}_{k}h),\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}\sum_{j\geq k+N_{0}}2^{2sj+\frac{3}{2}j+\frac{3}{2}k}|\mathfrak{F}_{k}h|_{L^{2}}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}f|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}|h|_{L^{2}}\sum_{j}2^{2sj+3j}|\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}f|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}|h|_{L^{2}}|f|_{H^{2s+4}}\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{1}|_{L^{2}}|f_{2}|_{L^{2}}|f_{3}|_{L^{2}}.

Combining the above estimates gives

\displaystyle|\langle Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(f_{2}f_{3},f_{1}),\mu^{1/2}\rangle|\lesssim C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|f_{1}|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}}|f_{2}|_{H^{s}}|f_{3}|_{H^{s}}.

By (
2.5
), since we can freely transfer
weight among
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then we put the negative order
-l
weight on each of
f_{1}f_{2},f_{3}
and the positive order
3l
weight on
\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}
to have the final estimate.
∎

2.6.
Upper bound of
\langle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta),f\rangle

Note that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta)=Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g\partial_{\beta}\mu^{1/2},h)+I^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta).

(2.48)

By Propositions
2.1
and
2.3
, we have the following
proposition.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
(a_{1},a_{2})=(\frac{3}{2}+\delta,s)
or
(s,\frac{3}{2}+\delta)
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\langle\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(g,h;\beta),f\rangle\lesssim_{l}C_{\delta,s,\gamma,\eta}|g|_{H^{a_{1}}_{-l}}|h|_{H^{a_{2}}_{-l}}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

2.7.
Upper bound of
\langle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f,h\rangle

Recall that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1})=\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,1}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1})+\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,1}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}):=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\partial_{\beta_{1}}\mu^{1/2},f;\beta_{0}),\quad\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}):=-\Gamma^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(f,\partial_{\beta_{1}}\mu^{1/2};\beta_{0}).

If
|\beta_{0}|=|\beta_{1}|=0
, the operators are reduced to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f=\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(f;0,0),\quad\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,1}f=\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,1}(f;0,0),\quad\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}f=\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}(f;0,0).

By Proposition
2.4
, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.5
.

It holds that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,1}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),h\rangle|\lesssim_{l}C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}|h|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

Note that
for any
0\leq\kappa,\iota\leq 1

\displaystyle(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2})(|v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2})\leq|v(\kappa)|^{2}+|v_{*}(\iota)|^{2}\leq(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2})(|v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2}),

which yields

\displaystyle\mu^{2}(v)\mu^{2}(v_{*})\leq\mu(v(\kappa))\mu(v_{*}(\iota))\leq\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}(v)\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}(v_{*}).

(2.49)

Thus, this estimate keeps a
\mu
-type weight in the upper bound of
\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}
.

Proposition 2.6
.

It holds that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),h\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|\mu^{1/32}f|_{H^{s}}|\mu^{1/32}h|_{H^{s}}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we only consider
|\beta_{0}|=|\beta_{1}|=0
.
Note that

\displaystyle\langle-\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta,2}f,h\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mu^{-1/2}Q^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}f,\mu),h\rangle=\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}f)_{*}\mu((\mu^{-1/2}h)^{\prime}-\mu^{-1/2}h)\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}f)_{*}\mu^{1/2}(h^{\prime}-h)\mathrm{d}V+\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f_{*}\mu^{1/2}h^{\prime}((\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}-\mu^{1/2}_{*})\mathrm{d}V:=Y_{1}+Y_{2}.

We first estimate
Y_{1}
. Observe that

\displaystyle Y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}f)_{*}\mu^{1/2}(h^{\prime}-h)\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}f)_{*}((\mu^{1/2}h)^{\prime}-\mu^{1/2}h)\mathrm{d}V+\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(\mu^{1/2}f)_{*}(\mu^{1/2}-(\mu^{1/2})^{\prime})h^{\prime}\mathrm{d}V:=Y_{1,1}+Y_{1,2}.

Proposition
2.2
implies

\displaystyle|Y_{1,1}|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|\mu^{1/2}f|_{H^{s}}|\mu^{1/2}h|_{H^{s}}.

By (
2.27
) and (
2.29
), using
(
2.49
), and the change of variable
v\to v^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle|Y_{1,2}|\lesssim\int\mathrm{1}_{|v-v_{*}|\leq 4\eta/3}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma+2}\mu^{1/12}_{*}f_{*}\mu^{1/12}h\mathrm{d}v_{*}\mathrm{d}v\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+5}}{\gamma+5}|\mu^{1/16}f|_{L^{2}}|\mu^{1/16}h|_{L^{2}}.

We now turn to
Y_{2}
. Note that

\displaystyle Y_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f_{*}\mu^{1/2}h^{\prime}((\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}-\mu^{1/2}_{*})\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f_{*}(\mu^{1/2}-(\mu^{1/2})^{\prime})h^{\prime}((\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}-\mu^{1/2}_{*})\mathrm{d}V+\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f_{*}(\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}h^{\prime}((\mu^{1/2})^{\prime}_{*}-\mu^{1/2}_{*})\mathrm{d}V

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle Y_{2,1}+Y_{2,2}.

Similar to the estimate on
Y_{1,2}
, we get

\displaystyle|Y_{2,2}|\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+5}}{\gamma+5}|\mu^{1/16}f|_{L^{2}}|\mu^{1/16}h|_{L^{2}}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle|Y_{2,1}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big(\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f^{2}_{*}(\mu^{1/4}-(\mu^{1/4})^{\prime})^{2}((\mu^{1/4})^{\prime}_{*}+\mu^{1/4}_{*})^{2}\mathrm{d}V\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\big(\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(h^{\prime})^{2}((\mu^{1/4})^{\prime}_{*}-\mu^{1/4}_{*})^{2}(\mu^{1/4}+(\mu^{1/4})^{\prime})^{2}\mathrm{d}V\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f^{2}_{*}(\mu^{1/4}-(\mu^{1/4})^{\prime})^{2}((\mu^{1/4})^{\prime}_{*}+\mu^{1/4}_{*})^{2}\mathrm{d}V\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\big(\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}h^{2}_{*}(\mu^{1/4}-(\mu^{1/4})^{\prime})^{2}((\mu^{1/4})^{\prime}_{*}+\mu^{1/4}_{*})^{2}\mathrm{d}V\big)^{1/2}.

By Taylor expansion,
using
(
2.49
) to obtain the
\mu
-type weight, we get

\displaystyle\int B^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}f^{2}_{*}(\mu^{1/4}-(\mu^{1/4})^{\prime})^{2}((\mu^{1/4})^{\prime}_{*}+\mu^{1/4}_{*})^{2}\mathrm{d}V\lesssim\frac{\eta^{\gamma+5}}{\gamma+5}|\mu^{1/32}f|^{2}_{L^{2}},

which gives

|Y_{2,1}|\lesssim(\gamma+5)^{-1}\eta^{\gamma+5}|\mu^{1/32}f|_{L^{2}}|\mu^{1/32}h|_{L^{2}}.

Combining the above estimates completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

By Propositions
2.5
and
2.6
, we also have the following
proposition.

Proposition 2.7
.

It holds that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{L}^{s,\gamma}_{\eta}(f;\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),h\rangle|\lesssim C_{s,\gamma,\eta}|f|_{H^{s}_{-l}}|h|_{H^{s}_{-l}}.

3.
Upper bound estimate in the regular region

In this section, we will derive some upper bound
estimates in the regular region
|v-v_{*}|\gtrsim\eta
.
Recall(
2.13
) that

\displaystyle\langle\Gamma^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h),f\rangle=\langle Q^{s,\gamma,\eta}(\mu^{1/2}g,h),f\rangle+\langle I^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h),f\rangle.

(3.1)

We now estimate
\langle Q^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h),f\rangle
and
\langle I^{s,\gamma,\eta}(g,h),f\rangle
in the following two subsections. With these estimates, we
will combine them suitably according to the operator splitting in subsection
2.2
to
obtain several operator estimates for later use.

3.1.
Upper bound for
Q^{s,\gamma,\eta}

Set
u=v-v_{*}
, the

v=v_{*}+u,v^{\prime}=v_{*}+u^{+}
where
u^{+}=\frac{|u|\sigma+u}{2}
. Define the translation operator
T_{v_{*}}
by
(T_{v_{*}}f)(v)=f(v_{*}+v)
. We recall the geometric decomposition into radial and spherical parts

\displaystyle f(v^{\prime})-f(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(((T_{v_{*}}f)(u^{+})-(T_{v_{*}}f)(|u|\frac{u^{+}}{|u^{+}|}))\right)+\left((T_{v_{*}}f)(|u|\frac{u^{+}}{|u^{+}|})-(T_{v_{*}}f)(u)\right)

(3.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{ radial part}+\text{ spherical part}.

3.1.1.
Radial part

We first derive some estimates on the radial part. Note that
|u^{+}-|u|\frac{u^{+}}{|u^{+}|}|=|u|(1-\cos\frac{\theta}{2})=2|u|\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{4}
, which yields an order-2 cancellation in the angular singularity. The radial part in (
3.2
) can be controlled by gain of
W_{s}
in the phase and frequency space, namely the two Sobolev norms
L^{2}_{s}
and
H^{s}
.
We show this by using some localization technique introduced in
[
24
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
0<\eta\leq 1
.
Set

\mathcal{Y}^{s,\gamma,\eta}(h,f):=\int b^{s}(\frac{u}{|u|}\cdot\sigma)|u|^{\gamma}\psi^{\eta}(u)h(u)[f(u^{+})-f(|u|\frac{u^{+}}{|u^{+}|})]\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}u,

then

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Y}^{s,\gamma,\eta}(h,f)|\lesssim s^{-1}\eta^{\gamma}(|W_{\gamma/2}h|_{L^{2}_{s}}+|W_{\gamma/2}h|_{H^{s}})(|W_{\gamma/2}f|_{L^{2}_{s}}+|W_{\gamma/2}f|_{H^{s}}).

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps.

Step 1: Without the term
|u|^{\gamma}\psi^{\eta}(u)
.
We first denote

\mathcal{X}(h,f):=\int b^{s}(\frac{u}{|u|}\cdot\sigma)h(u)[f(u^{+})-f(|u|\frac{u^{+}}{|u^{+}|})]\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}u.

By applying dyadic decomposition in the phase space, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(h,f)=\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\int b^{s}(\frac{u}{|u|}\cdot\sigma)(\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h)(u)[(\varphi_{k}f)(u^{+})-(\varphi_{k}f)(|u|\frac{u^{+}}{|u^{+}|})]\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}u:=\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{k}.

where
\tilde{\varphi}_{k}=\sum_{|l-k|\leq 3}\varphi_{l}
. By using Proposition 5.2 in
[
24
]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\frac{u}{|u|}\cdot\sigma)h(u)(\bar{f}(u^{+})-\bar{f}(\frac{|u|}{|u^{+}|}u^{+}))\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}u=\int_{\mathop{\mathbb{R}\kern 0.0pt}\nolimits^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)(\widehat{h}(\xi^{+})-\widehat{h}(\frac{|\xi|}{|\xi^{+}|}\xi^{+}))\overline{\widehat{f}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\xi.

(3.3)

By (
3.3
) and the dyadic decomposition in the frequency space, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int b^{s}(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)[\widehat{\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h}(\xi^{+})-\widehat{\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h}(|\xi|\frac{\xi^{+}}{|\xi^{+}|})]\overline{\widehat{\varphi_{k}f}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=-1}^{\infty}\int b^{s}(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)[({\varphi}_{l}\widehat{\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h})(\xi^{+})-({\varphi}_{l}\widehat{\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h})(|\xi|\frac{\xi^{+}}{|\xi^{+}|})](\tilde{\varphi}_{l}\overline{\widehat{\varphi_{k}f}})(\xi)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\xi:=\sum_{l=-1}^{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{k,l}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(h,f)=\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=-1}^{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{k,l}.

By dividing
\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
according to
\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\geq 2^{-\frac{k+l}{2}}
and
\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\leq 2^{-\frac{k+l}{2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{k,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int b^{s}(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\cdot\sigma)\mathrm{1}_{\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\geq 2^{-\frac{k+l}{2}}}[({\varphi}_{l}\widehat{\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h})(\xi^{+})-({\varphi}_{l}\widehat{\tilde{\varphi}_{k}h})(|\xi|\frac{\xi^{+}}{|\xi^{+}|})](\tilde{\varphi}_{l}\overline{\widehat{\varphi_{k}f}})(\xi)\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\xi
